<span class="atom-split" data-atom="the_two_noble_kinsmen" data-title="THE TWO NOBLE KINSMEN" data-chapter="" data-kind="intro"></span>


THE TWO NOBLE KINSMEN
BY JOHN FLETCHER AND WILLIAM SHAKESPEARE
WHEN it first appeared in print, in 1634, The Two Noble Kinsmen was stated to be ‘by the memorable worthies of their [[motifs/time|time]], Mr John Fletcher, and Mr William Shakespeare’. There is no reason to disbelieve this ascription: many plays of the period were not printed till long after they were acted, and there is other evidence that Shakespeare collaborated with Fletcher (1579―1625). The morris [[motifs/dance|dance]] in Act 3, Scene 5, contains characters who also appear in Francis Beaumont’s [[forms/masque|Masque]] of the Inner Temple and Gray’s Inn performed before James I on 20 February 1613. Their [[motifs/dance|dance]] was a great success with the [[archetypes/king|King]]; probably the [[archetypes/king|King]]’s Men—some of whom may have taken part in the [[forms/masque|masque]]—decided to exploit its success by incorporating it in a play written soon afterwards, in the last year of Shakespeare’s playwriting life.
The Two Noble Kinsmen, a [[forms/tragicomedy|tragicomedy]] of the kind that became popular during the last years of the first decade of the seventeenth century, is based on Chaucer’s Knight’s Tale, on which Shakespeare had already drawn for episodes of A Midsummer [[motifs/night|Night]]’s Dream. It tells a romantic tale of the conflicting claims of [[motifs/love|love]] and [[concepts/friendship|friendship]]: the ‘two noble kinsmen’, [[characters/palamon|Palamon]] and [[characters/arcite|Arcite]], are the closest of friends until each falls in [[motifs/love|love]] with [[characters/emilia|Emilia]], sister-in-law of [[characters/theseus|Theseus]], Duke of Athens. Their conflict is finally resolved by a formal combat with [[characters/emilia|Emilia]] as the prize, in which the loser is to be executed. [[characters/arcite|Arcite]] wins, and [[characters/palamon|Palamon]]’s head is on the block as news arrives that [[characters/arcite|Arcite]] has been thrown from his [[motifs/horse|horse]]. Dying, [[characters/arcite|Arcite]] commends [[characters/emilia|Emilia]] to his friend, and [[characters/theseus|Theseus]] rounds off the play with a meditation on the paradoxes of fortune.
Studies of style suggest that Shakespeare was primarily responsible for the rhetorically and ritualistically impressive Act 1, for Act 2, Scene 1. Act 3, Scenes 1 and 2; and for most of Act 5 (Scene 4 excepted), which includes emblematically spectacular episodes related to his other late plays. Fletcher appears mainly to have written the scenes showing the rivalry of [[characters/palamon|Palamon]] and [[characters/arcite|Arcite]] along with the sub-plots concerned with the Jailer’s daughter’s [[motifs/love|love]] for [[characters/palamon|Palamon]] and the rustics’ entertainment for [[characters/theseus|Theseus]].
Though the play was adapted by William Davenant as The Rivals (1664), its first known performances since the seventeenth century were at the Old Vic in 1928; it has been played only occasionally since then, but was chosen to open the Swan Theatre in Stratford-upon-Avon in 1986. Critical interest, too, has been slight; but Shakespeare’s contributions are entirely characteristic of his late style, and Fletcher’s scenes are both touching and funny.
THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY
PROLOGUE
[[characters/theseus|THESEUS]], Duke of Athens
[[characters/hippolyta|HIPPOLYTA]], [[characters/queen|Queen]] of the Amazons, later wife of [[characters/theseus|Theseus]]
[[characters/emilia|EMILIA]], her sister
PIRITHOUS, friend of [[characters/theseus|Theseus]]
Hymen, god of [[motifs/marriage|marriage]]
A BOY, who sings
ARTESIUS, an Athenian soldier
Three QUEENS, widows of kings killed in the siege of Thebes
VALERIUS, a Theban
A HERALD
WOMAN, attending [[characters/emilia|Emilia]]
An Athenian GENTLEMAN
MESSENGERS
Six KNIGHTS, three attending [[characters/arcite|Arcite]] and three [[characters/palamon|Palamon]]
A SERVANT
A JAILER in charge of [[characters/theseus|Theseus]]’ [[motifs/prison|prison]]
The JAILER’S DAUGHTER
The JAILER’S BROTHER
The WOOER of the Jailer’s daughter
Two FRIENDS of the Jailer
A DOCTOR
Six COUNTRYMEN, one dressed as a babion, or baboon
Gerald, a SCHOOLMASTER
NELL, a country wench
Four other country wenches: Friz, [[characters/madeline|Madeline]], Luce, and Barbara Timothy, a TABORER
EPILOGUE
Nymphs, attendants, maids, executioner, guard

The Two Noble Kinsmen
Prologue Flourish. Enter Prologue
PROLOGUE
New plays and maidenheads are near akin:
Much followed both, for both much money giv’n
If they stand sound and [[motifs/well|well]]. And a good play,
Whose modest scenes blush on his [[motifs/marriage|marriage]] day
And shake to lose his [[concepts/honour|honour]], is like her
That after holy tie and first [[motifs/night|night]]’s stir
Yet still is modesty, and still retains
More of the maid to sight than husband’s pains.
We pray our play may be so, for I am sure
It has a noble breeder and a pure,
A learned, and a poet never went
More famous yet ‘twixt Po and silver Trent.
Chaucer, of all admired, the story gives:
There constant to [[concepts/eternity|eternity]] it lives.
If we let fall the nobleness of this
And the first sound this [[archetypes/child|child]] hear be a hiss,
How will it shake the bones of that good man,
And make him cry from under ground, ‛O fan
From me the witless chaff of such a writer,
That blasts my bays and my famed works makes
lighter
Than [[characters/robin-hood|Robin Hood]]’? This is the [[concepts/fear|fear]] we bring,
For to say [[concepts/truth|truth]], it were an endless thing
And too ambitious to aspire to him,
Weak as we are, and almost breathless swim
In this deep [[motifs/water|water]]. Do but you hold out
Your helping hands and we shall tack about
And something do to save us. You shall hear
Scenes, though below his art, may yet appear
Worth two hours’ travail. To his bones, sweet [[motifs/sleep|sleep]];
Content to you. If this play do not keep
A little dull [[motifs/time|time]] from us, we perceive
Our losses fall so thick we must needs leave.
Flourish. Exit

| *(didascalia)* | Music. Enter Hymen with a torch burning, a Boy in a white robe before, singing and strewing [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]]. After Hymen, a nymph encompassed in her tresses, bearing a wheaten garland. Then [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]] between two other nymphs with wheaten chaplets on their heads. Then [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]], the bride, led by Pirithous and another holding a garland over her head, her tresses likewise hanging. After her, [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]] holding up her train. Then Artesius and other attendants |
| BOY | (sings during procession)<br>Roses, their sharp spines being gone,<br>Not royal in their smells alone,<br>But in their hue;<br>Maiden pinks, of odour faint,<br>Daisies smell-less, yet most quaint,<br>And sweet thyme true;<br>Primrose, first-born [[archetypes/child\|child]] of Ver,<br>Merry springtime’s harbinger,<br>With harebells dim;<br>Oxlips, in their cradles growing,<br>Marigolds, on deathbeds blowing,<br>Lark’s-heels trim;<br>All dear [[concepts/nature\|nature]]’s children sweet,<br>Lie fore bride and bridegroom’s feet,<br>He strews [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]]<br>Blessing their sense.<br>Not an angel of the air,<br>Bird melodious, or bird fair,<br>Is absent hence.<br>The crow, the sland’rous cuckoo, nor<br>The boding raven, nor chough hoar,<br>Nor chatt’ring pie,<br>May on our bridehouse perch or sing,<br>Or with them any discord bring,<br>But from it fly. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter three Queens in black, with veils stained, with imperial crowns. The First [[characters/queen\|Queen]] falls down at the foot of [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]]; the Second falls down at the foot of [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]]; the Third, before [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]] |
| FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | (to [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]])<br>For pity’s sake and true gentility’s,<br>Hear and respect me. |
| SECOND [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | (to [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]])<br>For your mother’s sake, And as you wish your womb may thrive with fair ones,<br>Hear and respect me. |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | (to [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]])<br>Now for the [[motifs/love\|love]] of him whom Jove hath marked<br>The [[concepts/honour\|honour]] of your bed, and for the sake<br>Of clear virginity, be advocate<br>For us and our distresses. This good deed<br>Shall raze you out o’th’ Book of Trespasses<br>All you are set down there. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | (to First [[characters/queen\|Queen]])<br>Sad lady, rise. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | (to Second [[characters/queen\|Queen]]) Stand up. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to Third [[characters/queen\|Queen]])<br>No knees to me. What woman I may stead that is distressed<br>Does bind me to her. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | (to First [[characters/queen\|Queen]])<br>What’s your request? Deliver you for all. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] kneeling still |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | We are three queens whose sovereigns fell before<br>The wrath of cruel Creon; who endured<br>The beaks of ravens, talons of the kites,<br>And pecks of crows in the foul fields of Thebes.<br>He will not suffer us to burn their bones,<br>To urn their ashes, nor to take th‘offence<br>Of mortal loathsomeness from the blest eye<br>Of holy Phoebus, but infects the winds<br>With stench of our slain lords. O pity, Duke!<br>Thou purger of the earth, draw thy feared sword<br>That does good turns to’th’ world; give us the bones<br>Of our dead kings that we may chapel them;<br>And of thy boundless goodness take some note<br>That for our crowned heads we have no roof,<br>Save this, which is the [[motifs/lion\|lion]]’s and the bear’s,<br>And vault to everything. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Pray you, kneel not: I was transported with your speech, and suffered<br>Your knees to wrong themselves. I have heard the<br>fortunes<br>Of your dead lords, which gives me such lamenting<br>As wakes my vengeance and [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] for ’em.<br>[[archetypes/king\|King]] Capaneus was your lord: the day<br>That he should marry you—at such a season<br>As now it is with me—I met your groom<br>By Mars’s altar. You were that [[motifs/time\|time]] fair,<br>Not Juno’s mantle fairer than your tresses,<br>Nor in more bounty spread her. Your wheaten wreath<br>Was then nor threshed nor blasted; fortune at you 6<br>Dimpled her cheek with smiles; Hercules our<br>kinsman—<br>Then weaker than your eyes—laid by his club.<br>He tumbled down upon his Nemean hide<br>And swore his sinews thawed. O grief and [[motifs/time\|time]],<br>Fearful consumers, you will all devour. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] kneeling still |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | O, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] some god, Some god hath put his [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]] in your manhood,<br>Whereto he’ll infuse power and press you forth<br>Our undertaker. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | O no knees, none, widow: |
| *(didascalia)* | The First [[characters/queen\|Queen]] |
| *(didascalia)* | rises |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Unto the helmeted Bellona use them<br>And pray for me, your soldier. Troubled I am.<br>He turns away |
| *(didascalia)* | SECOND [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] kneeling still |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Honoured [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]], Most dreaded Amazonian, that hast slain<br>The scythe-tusked boar, that with thy arm, as strong<br>As it is white, wast near to make the male<br>To thy sex captive, but that this, thy lord—<br>Born to uphold creation in that [[concepts/honour\|honour]]<br>First [[concepts/nature\|nature]] styled it in—shrunk thee into<br>The bound thou wast o‘erflowing, at once subduing<br>Thy force and thy affection; soldieress,<br>That equally canst poise sternness with pity,<br>Whom now I know hast much more power on him<br>Than ever he had on thee, who ow’st his strength,<br>And his [[motifs/love\|love]] too, who is a servant for<br>The tenor of thy speech; dear glass of ladies,<br>Bid him that we, whom flaming war doth scorch,<br>Under the [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]] of his sword may cool us.<br>Require him he advance it o‘er our heads.<br>Speak’t in a woman’s key, like such a woman<br>As any of us three. Weep ere you fail.<br>Lend us a knee:<br>But touch the ground for us no longer [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>Than a dove’s motion when the head’s plucked off.<br>Tell him, if he i’th’ [[motifs/blood\|blood]]-sized field lay swoll’n,<br>Showing the sun his teeth, grinning at the [[motifs/moon\|moon]],<br>What you would do. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | Poor lady, say no more.<br>I had as lief trace this good action with you<br>As that whereto I am going, and never yet<br>Went I so willing way. My lord is taken<br>Heart-deep with your distress. Let him consider.<br>I’ll speak anon. |
| *(didascalia)* | The Second [[characters/queen\|Queen]] rises |
| *(didascalia)* | THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] (kneeling still to [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]]) |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | O, my petition was<br>Set down in ice, which by hot grief uncandied<br>Melts into drops; so sorrow, wanting form,<br>Is pressed with deeper matter. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | Pray stand up:<br>Your grief is written in your cheek. |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | O woe,<br>You cannot read it there; there, through my [[motifs/tears\|tears]],<br>Like wrinkled pebbles in a glassy stream,<br>You may behold ’em. |
| *(didascalia)* | The |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | Third [[characters/queen\|Queen]] |
| *(didascalia)* | rises |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | Lady, lady, alack—<br>He that will all the treasure know o’th’ earth<br>Must know the centre too; he that will fish<br>For my least minnow, let him lead his line<br>To catch one at my heart. O, pardon me:<br>Extremity, that sharpens sundry wits,<br>Makes me a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | Pray you, say nothing, pray you.<br>Who cannot feel nor see the rain, being in’t,<br>Knows neither wet nor dry. If that you were<br>The ground-piece of some painter, I would buy you<br>T’instruct me ’gainst a capital grief, indeed<br>Such heart-pierced demonstration; but, alas,<br>Being a natural sister of our sex,<br>Your sorrow beats so ardently upon me<br>That it shall make a counter-reflect ’gainst<br>My brother’s heart, and warm it to some pity,<br>Though it were made of stone. Pray have good<br>comfort. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Forward to th’ temple. Leave not out a jot<br>O’th’ sacred ceremony. |
| FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | O, this celebration<br>Will longer last and be more costly than<br>Your suppliants’ war. Remember that your fame<br>Knolls in the ear o’th’ world: what you do quickly<br>Is not done rashly; your first thought is more<br>Than others’ laboured meditance; your premeditating<br>More than their actions. But, O Jove, your actions,<br>Soon as they move, as ospreys do the fish,<br>Subdue before they touch. Think, dear Duke, think<br>What beds our slain kings have. |
| SECOND [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | What griefs our beds,<br>That our dear lords have none. |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | None fit for th’ dead.<br>Those that with cords, knives, drams, precipitance,<br>Weary of this world’s light, have to themselves<br>Been [[motifs/death\|death]]’s most horrid agents, human grace<br>Affords them [[motifs/dust\|dust]] and [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]]. |
| FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | But our lords<br>Lie blist’ring fore the visitating sun,<br>And were good kings, when living. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | It is true,<br>And I will give you comfort to give your dead lords<br>graves,<br>The which to do must make some work with Creon. |
| FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | And that work presents itself to th’ doing.<br>Now ’twill take form, the heats are gone tomorrow.<br>Then, bootless toil must recompense itself<br>With its own sweat; now he’s secure,<br>Not [[motifs/dreams\|dreams]] we stand before your puissance<br>Rinsing our holy begging in our eyes<br>To make petition clear. |
| SECOND [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | Now you may take him,<br>Drunk with his victory. |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | And his army full<br>Of bread and sloth. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Artesius, that best knowest How to draw out, fit to this enterprise<br>The prim’st for this proceeding and the number<br>To carry such a business: forth and levy<br>Our worthiest instruments, whilst we dispatch<br>This grand act of our life, this daring deed<br>Of fate in wedlock. |
| FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | (to the other two Queens)<br>Dowagers, take hands;<br>Let us be widows to our woes; delay<br>Commends us to a famishing [[concepts/hope\|hope]]. |
| ALL THREE QUEENS | Farewell. |
| SECOND [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | We come unseasonably, but when could grief<br>Cull forth, as unpanged judgement can, fitt’st [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>For best solicitation? |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Why, good ladies,<br>This is a service whereto I am going<br>Greater than any war—it more imports me<br>Than all the actions that I have foregone,<br>Or futurely can cope. |
| FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | The more proclaiming<br>Our suit shall be neglected when her arms,<br>Able to lock Jove from a synod, shall<br>By warranting moonlight corslet thee! O when<br>Her twinning cherries shall their sweetness fall<br>Upon thy tasteful lips, what wilt thou think<br>Of rotten kings or blubbered queens? What care<br>For what thou feel’st not, what thou feel’st being able<br>To make Mars spurn his drum? O, if thou couch<br>But one [[motifs/night\|night]] with her, every hour in’t will<br>Take hostage of thee for a hundred, and<br>Thou shalt remember nothing more than what<br>That banquet bids thee to. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | (to [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]])<br>Though much unlike<br>You should be so transported, as much sorry<br>I should be such a suitor—yet I think<br>Did I not by th’abstaining of my joy,<br>Which breeds a deeper [[concepts/longing\|longing]], cure their surfeit<br>That craves a present medicine, I should pluck |
| *(didascalia)* | All ladies’ scandal on me. Kneels Therefore, sir, |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | As I shall here make trial of my prayers,<br>Either presuming them to have some force,<br>Or sentencing for aye their vigour dumb,<br>Prorogue this business we are going about, and hang<br>Your shield afore your heart—about that neck<br>Which is my fee, and which I freely lend<br>To do these poor queens service. |
| ALL THREE QUEENS | (to [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]])<br>O, help now,<br>Our cause cries for your knee. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | (kneels to [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]])<br>If you grant not<br>My sister her petition in that force<br>With that celerity and [[concepts/nature\|nature]] which<br>She makes it in, from henceforth I’ll not dare<br>To ask you anything, nor be so hardy<br>Ever to take a husband. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Pray stand up. |
| *(didascalia)* | They rise |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | I am entreating of myself to do<br>That which you kneel to have me.—Pirithous,<br>Lead on the bride: get you and pray the gods<br>For success and return; omit not anything<br>In the pretended celebration.—Queens,<br>Follow your soldier. (To Artesius) As before, hence you,<br>And at the banks of Aulis meet us with<br>The forces you can raise, where we shall find<br>The moiety of a number for a business<br>More bigger looked. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Artesius |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | (To [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]]) Since that our theme is haste,<br>I stamp this kiss upon thy current lip—<br>Sweet, keep it as my token. (To the wedding party) Set<br>you forward,<br>For I will see you gone.<br>(To [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]]) Farewell, my beauteous sister.—Pirithous,<br>Keep the feast full: bate not an hour on’t. |
| PIRITHOUS | Sir,<br>I’ll follow you at heels. The feast’s solemnity<br>Shall want till your return. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Cousin, I charge you<br>Budge not from Athens. We shall be returning<br>Ere you can end this feast, of which, I pray you,<br>Make no abatement.—Once more, farewell all. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]], [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]], Pirithous, and train towards the temple |
| FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | Thus dost thou still make good the tongue o’th’ world. |
| SECOND [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | And earn’st a deity equal with Mars— |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | If not above him, for Thou being but mortal mak’st affections bend<br>To godlike honours; they themselves, some say,<br>Groan under such a mast’ry. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | As we are men,<br>Thus should we do; being sensually subdued<br>We lose our human title. Good cheer, ladies.<br>Now turn we towards your comforts. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] and |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Dear [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]], dearer in [[motifs/love\|love]] than [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>And our prime cousin, yet unhardened in<br>The crimes of [[concepts/nature\|nature]], let us leave the [[motifs/city\|city]],<br>Thebes, and the temptings in’t, before we further<br>Sully our gloss of youth.<br>And here to keep in abstinence we shame<br>As in incontinence; for not to swim<br>I’th’ aid o’th’ current were almost to sink—<br>At least to frustrate striving; and to follow<br>The common stream ’twould bring us to an eddy<br>Where we should turn or drown; if labour through,<br>Our gain but life and weakness. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Your advice<br>Is cried up with example. What strange ruins<br>Since first we went to school may we perceive<br>Walking in Thebes? Scars and bare weeds<br>The gain o’th’ martialist who did propound<br>To his bold ends [[concepts/honour\|honour]] and golden ingots,<br>Which though he won, he had not; and now flirted<br>By peace for whom he fought. Who then shall offer<br>To Mars’s so-scorned altar? I do bleed<br>When such I meet, and wish great Juno would<br>Resume her ancient fit of [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]]<br>To get the soldier work, that peace might purge<br>For her repletion and retain anew<br>Her charitable heart, now hard and harsher<br>Than strife or war could be. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Are you not out?<br>Meet you no ruin but the soldier in<br>The cranks and turns of Thebes? You did begin<br>As if you met decays of many kinds.<br>Perceive you none that do arouse your pity<br>But th’unconsidered soldier? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes, I pity<br>Decays where’er I find them, but such most<br>That, sweating in an honourable toil,<br>Are paid with ice to cool ’em. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | ’Tis not this<br>I did begin to speak of. This is virtue,<br>Of no respect in Thebes. I spake of Thebes,<br>How dangerous, if we will keep our honours,<br>It is for our residing where every evil<br>Hath a good colour, where every seeming good’s<br>A certain evil, where not to be ev’n jump<br>As they are here were to be strangers, and<br>Such things to be, mere monsters. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | ’Tis in our power,<br>Unless we [[concepts/fear\|fear]] that apes can tutor’s, to<br>Be masters of our manners. What need I<br>Affect another’s gait, which is not catching<br>Where there is [[concepts/faith\|faith]]? Or to be fond upon<br>Another’s way of speech, when by mine own<br>I may be reasonably conceived—saved, too—<br>Speaking it truly? Why am I bound<br>By any generous bond to follow him<br>Follows his tailor, haply so long until<br>The followed make pursuit? Or let me know<br>Why mine own barber is unblest—with him<br>My poor chin, too—for ’tis not scissored just<br>To such a favourite’s glass? What canon is there<br>That does command my rapier from my hip<br>To dangle’t in my hand? Or to go tiptoe<br>Before the street be foul? Either I am<br>The fore-[[motifs/horse\|horse]] in the team or I am none<br>That draw i’th’ sequent trace. These poor slight<br>sores<br>Need not a plantain. That which rips my bosom<br>Almost to th’ heart’s— |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Our uncle Creon. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | He,<br>A most unbounded [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]], whose successes<br>Makes heaven unfeared and villainy assured<br>Beyond its power there’s nothing; almost puts<br>[[concepts/faith\|Faith]] in a fever, and deifies alone<br>Voluble chance; who only attributes<br>The faculties of other instruments<br>To his own nerves and act; commands men’s service,<br>And what they win in’t, boot and glory; one<br>That fears not to do harm, good dares not. Let<br>The [[motifs/blood\|blood]] of mine that’s sib to him be sucked<br>From me with leeches. Let them break and fall<br>Off me with that corruption. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Clear-spirited cousin,<br>Let’s leave his [[settings/court\|court]] that we may nothing share<br>Of his loud infamy: for our milk<br>Will relish of the pasture, and we must<br>Be vile or disobedient; not his kinsmen<br>In [[motifs/blood\|blood]] unless in quality. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Nothing truer.<br>I think the echoes of his shames have deafed<br>The ears of heav’nly justice. Widows’ cries<br>Descend again into their throats and have not |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Valerius |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Due audience of the gods—Valerius. |
| VALERIUS | The [[archetypes/king\|King]] calls for you; yet be leaden-footed<br>Till his great rage be off him. Phoebus, when<br>He broke his whipstock and exclaimed against<br>The horses of the sun, but whispered to<br>The loudness of his fury. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Small winds shake him.<br>But what’s the matter? |
| VALERIUS | [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]], who where he threats, appals, hath sent<br>Deadly defiance to him and pronounces<br>Ruin to Thebes, who is at hand to seal<br>The promise of his wrath. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Let him approach.<br>But that we [[concepts/fear\|fear]] the gods in him, he brings not<br>A jot of terror to us. Yet what man<br>Thirds his own worth—the case is each of ours—<br>When that his action’s dregged with mind assured<br>’Tis bad he goes about. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Leave that unreasoned.<br>Our services stand now for Thebes, not Creon,<br>Yet to be neutral to him were dishonour,<br>Rebellious to oppose. Therefore we must<br>With him stand to the [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]] of our fate,<br>Who hath bounded our last minute. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | So we must.<br>Is’t said this war’s afoot? Or it shall be<br>On fail of some condition? |
| VALERIUS | ’Tis in motion,<br>The intelligence of state came in the instant<br>With the defier. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Let’s to the [[archetypes/king\|King]], who, were he A quarter carrier of that [[concepts/honour\|honour]] which<br>His enemy come in, the [[motifs/blood\|blood]] we venture<br>Should be as for our health, which were not spent,<br>Rather laid out for purchase. But, alas,<br>Our hands advanced before our hearts, what will<br>The fall o’th’ stroke do damage? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Let th’event—That never-erring arbitrator—tell us<br>When we know all ourselves, and let us follow<br>The becking of our chance. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Pirithous, [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]], and [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]] |
| PIRITHOUS | No further. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | Sir, farewell. Repeat my wishes To our great lord, of whose success I dare not<br>Make any timorous question; yet I wish him<br>Excess and overflow of power, an’t might be,<br>To dure ill-dealing fortune. [[characters/speed\|Speed]] to him;<br>Store never hurts good governors. |
| PIRITHOUS | Though I know His ocean needs not my poor drops, yet they<br>Must yield their tribute there. (To [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]]) My precious<br>maid,<br>Those best affections that the heavens infuse<br>In their best-tempered pieces keep enthroned<br>In your dear heart. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | Thanks, sir. Remember me To our all-royal brother, for whose [[characters/speed\|speed]]<br>The great Bellona I’ll solicit; and<br>Since in our terrene state petitions are not<br>Without gifts understood, I’ll offer to her<br>What I shall be advised she likes. Our hearts<br>Are in his army, in his tent. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | In’s bosom.<br>We have been soldiers, and we cannot weep<br>When our friends don their helms, or put to sea,<br>Or tell of babes broached on the lance, or women<br>That have sod their infants in—and after eat them—<br>The brine they wept at killing ’em: then if<br>You stay to see of us such spinsters, we<br>Should hold you here forever. |
| PIRITHOUS | Peace be to you As I pursue this war, which shall be then<br>Beyond further requiring. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Pirithous |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | How his [[concepts/longing\|longing]] Follows his friend! Since his depart, his sports,<br>Though craving seriousness and skill, passed slightly<br>His careless execution, where nor gain<br>Made him regard or loss consider, but<br>Playing one business in his hand, another<br>Directing in his head, his mind [[characters/nurse\|nurse]] equal<br>To these so diff’ring twins. Have you observed him<br>Since our great lord departed? |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | With much labour; And I did [[motifs/love\|love]] him for’t. They two have cabined<br>In many as dangerous as poor a corner,<br>Peril and want contending; they have skiffed<br>Torrents whose roaring tyranny and power<br>I’th’ least of these was dreadful, and they have<br>Fought out together where [[motifs/death\|death]]’s [[concepts/self\|self]] was lodged;<br>Yet fate hath brought them off. Their knot of [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>Tied, weaved, entangled with so true, so long,<br>And with a finger of so deep a cunning,<br>May be outworn, never undone. I think<br>[[characters/theseus\|Theseus]] cannot be umpire to himself,<br>Cleaving his [[concepts/conscience\|conscience]] into twain and doing<br>Each side like justice, which he loves best. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | Doubtless There is a best, and reason has no manners<br>To say it is not you. I was acquainted<br>Once with a [[motifs/time\|time]] when I enjoyed a playfellow;<br>You were at wars when she the [[motifs/grave\|grave]] enriched,<br>Who made too proud the bed; took leave o’th’<br>[[motifs/moon\|moon]]—<br>Which then looked pale at parting—when our count<br>Was each eleven. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | ’Twas Flavina. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | Yes.<br>You talk of Pirithous’ and [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]]’ [[motifs/love\|love]]:<br>Theirs has more ground, is more maturely seasoned,<br>More buckled with strong judgement, and their needs<br>The one of th‘other may be said to [[motifs/water\|water]]<br>Their intertangled roots of [[motifs/love\|love]]; but I<br>And she I sigh and spoke of were things [[archetypes/innocent\|innocent]],<br>Loved for we did, and like the elements,<br>That know not what, nor why, yet do effect<br>Rare issues by their operance, our souls<br>Did so to one another. What she liked<br>Was then of me approved; what not, condemned—<br>No more arraignment. The flower that I would pluck<br>And put between my breasts—O then but beginning<br>To swell about the blossom—she would long<br>Till she had such another, and commit it<br>To the like [[archetypes/innocent\|innocent]] cradle, where, phoenix-like,<br>They died in perfume. On my head no toy<br>But was her pattern. Her affections—pretty,<br>Though happily her careless wear—I followed<br>For my most serious decking. Had mine ear<br>Stol’n some new air, or at adventure hummed one,<br>From musical coinage, why, it was a note<br>Whereon her spirits would sojourn—rather dwell on—<br>And sing it in her slumbers. This rehearsal—<br>Which, seely innocence wots [[motifs/well\|well]], comes in<br>Like old emportment’s bastard—has this end:<br>That the true [[motifs/love\|love]] ’tween maid and maid may be<br>More than in sex dividual. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | You’re out of breath, And this high-speeded pace is but to say<br>That you shall never, like the maid Flavina,<br>[[motifs/love\|Love]] any that’s called man. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | I am sure I shall not. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | Now alack, weak sister, I must no more believe thee in this point—<br>Though in’t I know thou dost believe thyself—<br>Than I will trust a sickly appetite<br>That loathes even as it longs. But sure, my sister,<br>If I were ripe for your persuasion, you<br>Have said enough to shake me from the arm<br>Of the all-noble [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]], for whose fortunes<br>I will now in and kneel, with great assurance<br>That we more than his Pirithous possess<br>The high throne in his heart. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | I am not<br>Against your [[concepts/faith\|faith]], yet I continue mine. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Cornetts. A battle struck within. Then a retreat. Flourish. Then enter [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]], victor. The three Queens meet him and fall on their faces before him. Also enter a Herald, and attendants bearing [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] and [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] on two hearses |
| FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | (to [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]])<br>To thee no star be dark. |
| SECOND [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | (to [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]]) Both heaven and earth<br>Friend thee for ever. |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | (to [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]]) All the good that may Be wished upon thy head, I cry ‘Amen’ to’t. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Th’impartial gods, who from the mounted heavens<br>View us their mortal herd, behold who err<br>And in their [[motifs/time\|time]] chastise. Go and find out<br>The bones of your dead lords and [[concepts/honour\|honour]] them<br>With treble ceremony: rather than a gap<br>Should be in their dear rites we would supply’t.<br>But those we will depute which shall invest<br>You in your dignities, and even each thing<br>Our haste does leave imperfect. So adieu,<br>And heaven’s good eyes look on you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the Queens |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | What are those? |
| HERALD | Men of great quality, as may be judged<br>By their appointment. Some of Thebes have told’s<br>They are sisters’ children, nephews to the [[archetypes/king\|King]]. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | By th’ helm of Mars I saw them in the war,<br>Like to a pair of lions smeared with prey,<br>Make lanes in troops aghast. I fixed my note<br>Constantly on them, for they were a mark<br>Worth a god’s view. What prisoner was’t that told me<br>When I enquired their names? |
| HERALD | Wi’ leave, they’re called [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] and [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]]. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | ’Tis right: those, those. They are not dead? |
| HERALD | Nor in a state of life. Had they been taken<br>When their last hurts were given, ’twas possible<br>They might have been recovered. Yet they breathe,<br>And have the name of men. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Then like men use ‘em. The very lees of such, millions of rates<br>Exceed the wine of others. All our surgeons<br>Convent in their behoof; our richest balms,<br>Rather than niggard, waste. Their lives concern us<br>Much more than Thebes is worth. Rather than have<br>’em<br>Freed of this plight and in their morning state— |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound and at liberty—I would ’em dead; |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | But forty-thousandfold we had rather have ’em<br>Prisoners to us, than [[motifs/death\|death]]. Bear ‘em speedily<br>From our kind air, to them unkind, and minister<br>What man to man may do-for our sake, more,<br>Since I have known frights, fury, friends’ behests,<br>[[motifs/love\|Love]]’s provocations, zeal, a mistress’ task,<br>[[motifs/desire\|Desire]] of liberty, a fever, [[concepts/madness\|madness]],<br>Hath set a mark which [[concepts/nature\|nature]] could not reach to<br>Without some imposition, sickness in will<br>O’er-wrestling strength in reason. For our [[motifs/love\|love]]<br>And great [[characters/apollo\|Apollo]]’s [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]], all our best<br>Their best skill tender.—Lead into the [[motifs/city\|city]]<br>Where, having bound things scattered, we will post<br>To Athens fore our army. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Music. Enter the three Queens with the hearses of their lords in a funeral solemnity, with attendants |
| *(didascalia)* | [[works/song-(poe)\|Song]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Urns and odours, bring away,<br>Vapours, sighs, darken the day;<br>Our dole more deadly looks than dying.<br>Balms and gums and heavy cheers,<br>Sacred vials filled with [[motifs/tears\|tears]],<br>And clamours through the wild air flying:<br><br>Come all sad and solemn shows,<br>That are quick-eyed pleasure’s foes.<br>We convent naught else but woes,<br>We convent naught else but woes. |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | This funeral path brings to your household’s [[motifs/grave\|grave]]—<br>Joy seize on you again, peace [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]] with him. |
| SECOND [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | And this to yours. |
| FIRST [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | Yours this way. Heavens lend A thousand differing ways to one sure end. |
| THIRD [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | This world’s a [[motifs/city\|city]] full of straying streets,<br>And [[motifs/death\|death]]’s the market-place where each one meets. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 2, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Jailer and the Wooer |
| JAILER | I may depart with little, while I live; something I may cast to you, not much. Alas, the [[motifs/prison\|prison]] I keep, though it be for great ones, yet they seldom come; before one salmon you shall take a number of minnows. I am given out to be better lined than it can appear to me report is a true speaker. I would I were really that I am delivered to be. Marry, what I have—be it what it will—I will assure upon my daughter at the day of my [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| WOOER | Sir, I demand no more than your own offer, and I will estate your daughter in what I have promised. |
| JAILER | [[motifs/well\|Well]], we will talk more of this when the solemnity is past. But have you a full promise of her? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Jailer’s Daughter with rushes |
| JAILER | When that shall be seen, I tender my consent. |
| WOOER | I have, sir. Here she comes. |
| JAILER | (to Daughter) Your friend and I have chanced to name you here, upon the old business—but no more of that now. So soon as the [[settings/court\|court]] hurry is over we will have an end of it. I’th’ mean [[motifs/time\|time]], look tenderly to the two prisoners. I can tell you they are princes. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | These strewings are for their chamber. ’Tis pity they are in [[motifs/prison\|prison]], and ’twere pity they should be out. I do think they have patience to make any adversity ashamed; the [[motifs/prison\|prison]] itself is proud of ’em, and they have all the world in their chamber. |
| JAILER | They are famed to be a pair of absolute men. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | By my troth, I think fame but stammers ’em—they stand a grece above the reach of report. |
| JAILER | I heard them reported in the battle to be the only doers. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | Nay, most likely, for they are noble sufferers. I marvel how they would have looked had they been victors, that with such a constant nobility enforce a [[concepts/freedom\|freedom]] out of bondage, making misery their mirth, and affliction a toy to jest at. |
| JAILER | Do they so? |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | It seems to me they have no more sense of their captivity than I of ruling Athens. They eat [[motifs/well\|well]], look merrily, discourse of many things, but nothing of their own restraint and disasters. Yet sometime a divided sigh—martyred as ’twere i’th’ deliverance—will break from one of them, when the other presently gives it so sweet a rebuke that I could wish myself a sigh to be so chid, or at least a sigher to be comforted. |
| WOOER | I never saw ’em. |
| JAILER | The Duke himself came privately in the [[motifs/night\|night]], |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] and [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] appear at a [[motifs/window\|window]] above |
| JAILER | and so did they. What the reason of it is I know not. Look, yonder they are. That’s [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] looks out. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | No, sir, no—that’s [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]]. [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] is the lower of the twain—(pointing at [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]) you may perceive a part of him. |
| JAILER | Go to, leave your pointing. They would not make us their object. Out of their sight. |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | It is a holiday to look on them. Lord, the difference of men! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] and [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] in [[motifs/prison\|prison]], in shackles, above |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | How do you, noble cousin? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | How do you, sir? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Why, strong enough to laugh at misery<br>And bear the chance of war. Yet we are prisoners,<br>I [[concepts/fear\|fear]], for ever, cousin. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | I believe it, And to that destiny have patiently<br>Laid up my hour to come. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | O, cousin [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]], Where is Thebes now? Where is our noble country?<br>Where are our friends and kindreds? Never more<br>Must we behold those comforts, never see<br>The hardy youths strive for the games of [[concepts/honour\|honour]],<br>Hung with the painted favours of their ladies,<br>Like tall ships under sail; then start amongst ’em<br>And, as an east [[motifs/wind\|wind]], leave ’em all behind us,<br>Like lazy clouds, whilst [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] and [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]],<br>Even in the wagging of a wanton leg,<br>Outstripped the people’s praises, won the garlands<br>Ere they have [[motifs/time\|time]] to wish ‘em ours. O never<br>Shall we two exercise, like twins of [[concepts/honour\|honour]],<br>Our arms again and feel our fiery horses<br>Like proud seas under us. Our good swords, now—<br>Better the red-eyed god of war ne’er wore—<br>Ravished our sides, like age must run to rust<br>And deck the temples of those gods that hate us.<br>These hands shall never draw ’em out like [[motifs/lightning\|lightning]]<br>To blast whole armies more. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | No, [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]], Those hopes are prisoners with us. Here we are,<br>And here the graces of our youths must wither,<br>Like a too-timely [[motifs/spring\|spring]]. Here age must find us<br>And, which is heaviest, [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]], unmarried—<br>The sweet embraces of a loving wife<br>Loaden with kisses, armed with thousand Cupids,<br>Shall never clasp our necks; no issue know us;<br>No figures of ourselves shall we e’er see<br>To glad our age, and, like young eagles, teach ’em<br>Boldly to gaze against bright arms and say,<br>‘Remember what your fathers were, and conquer.’<br>The fair-eyed maids shall weep our banishments,<br>And in their songs curse ever-blinded fortune,<br>Till she for shame see what a wrong she has done<br>To youth and [[concepts/nature\|nature]]. This is all our world.<br>We shall know nothing here but one another,<br>Hear nothing but the [[motifs/clock\|clock]] that tells our woes.<br>The vine shall grow, but we shall never see it;<br>[[motifs/summer\|Summer]] shall come, and with her all delights,<br>But dead-cold [[motifs/winter\|winter]] must inhabit here still. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | ’Tis too true, [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]. To our Theban hounds<br>That shook the aged forest with their echoes,<br>No more now must we holler; no more shake<br>Our pointed javelins whilst the angry swine<br>Flies like a Parthian quiver from our rages,<br>Struck with our [[motifs/well\|well]]-steeled darts. All valiant uses—<br>The food and nourishment of noble minds—<br>In us two here shall perish; we shall die—<br>Which is the curse of [[concepts/honour\|honour]]—lastly,<br>Children of grief and ignorance. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yet, cousin, Even from the [[characters/bottom\|bottom]] of these miseries,<br>From all that fortune can inflict upon us,<br>I see two comforts rising—two mere blessings,<br>If the gods please, to hold here a brave patience<br>And the enjoying of our griefs together.<br>Whilst [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] is with me, let me perish<br>If I think this our [[motifs/prison\|prison]]. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Certainly ’Tis a main goodness, cousin, that our fortunes<br>Were twined together. ’Tis most true, two souls<br>Put in two noble bodies, let ’em suffer<br>The gall of hazard, so they grow together,<br>Will never sink; they must not, say they could.<br>A willing man dies sleeping and all’s done. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Shall we make worthy uses of this place<br>That all men hate so much? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | How, gentle cousin? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Let’s think this [[motifs/prison\|prison]] holy sanctuary,<br>To keep us from corruption of worse men.<br>We are young, and yet [[motifs/desire\|desire]] the ways of [[concepts/honour\|honour]]<br>That liberty and common conversation,<br>The [[motifs/poison\|poison]] of pure spirits, might, like women,<br>Woo us to wander from. What worthy blessing<br>Can be, but our imaginations<br>May make it ours? And here being thus together,<br>We are an endless mine to one another:<br>We are one another’s wife, ever begetting<br>New births of [[motifs/love\|love]]; we are father, friends,<br>acquaintance;<br>We are in one another, families—<br>I am your heir, and you are mine; this place<br>Is our inheritance: no hard oppressor<br>Dare take this from us. Here, with a little patience,<br>We shall live long and loving. No surfeits seek us—<br>The hand of war hurts none here, nor the seas<br>[[motifs/swallow\|Swallow]] their youth. Were we at liberty<br>A wife might part us lawfully, or business;<br>Quarrels consume us; envy of ill men<br>Crave our acquaintance. I might sicken, cousin,<br>Where you should never know it, and so perish<br>Without your noble hand to close mine eyes,<br>Or prayers to the gods. A thousand chances,<br>Were we from hence, would sever us. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | You have made me—I thank you, cousin [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]—almost wanton<br>With my captivity. What a misery<br>It is to live abroad, and everywhere!<br>’Tis like a beast, methinks. I find the [[settings/court\|court]] here;<br>I am sure, a more content; and all those pleasures<br>That woo the wills of men to [[concepts/vanity\|vanity]]<br>I see through now, and am sufficient<br>To tell the world ’tis but a gaudy [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]],<br>That old [[motifs/time\|Time]], as he passes by, takes with him.<br>What had we been, old in the [[settings/court\|court]] of Creon,<br>Where sin is justice, lust and ignorance<br>The virtues of the great ones? Cousin [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]],<br>Had not the loving gods found this place for us,<br>We had died as they do, ill old men, unwept,<br>And had their epitaphs, the people’s curses.<br>Shall I say more? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | I would hear you still. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Ye shall. Is there record of any two that loved<br>Better than we do, [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Sure there cannot. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | I do not think it possible our [[concepts/friendship\|friendship]]<br>Should ever leave us. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Till our deaths it cannot, |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]] and her Woman below. [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] sees [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]] and is silent |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | And after [[motifs/death\|death]] our spirits shall be led<br>To those that [[motifs/love\|love]] eternally. Speak on, sir. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman)<br>This [[motifs/garden\|garden]] has a world of pleasure in’t.<br>What flower is this? |
| WOMAN | ’Tis called narcissus, madam. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | That was a fair boy, certain, but a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]<br>To [[motifs/love\|love]] himself. Were there not maids enough? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | (to [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]])<br>Pray forward. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman) Or were they all hard-hearted? |
| WOMAN | They could not be to one so fair. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | Thou wouldst not. |
| WOMAN | I think I should not, madam. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | That’s a good wench—But take heed to your kindness, though. |
| WOMAN | Why, madam? |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | Men are mad things. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | (to [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]]) Will ye go forward, cousin? |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman)<br>Canst not thou work such [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]] in silk, wench? |
| WOMAN | Yes. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | I’ll have a gown full of ’em, and of these.<br>This is a pretty colour—will’t not do<br>Rarely upon a skirt, wench? |
| WOMAN | Dainty, madam. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | (to [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]])<br>Cousin, cousin, how do you, sir? Why, [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]]! |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Never till now was I in [[motifs/prison\|prison]], [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Why, what’s the matter, man? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Behold and [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]]!<br>[[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] sees [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]]<br>By heaven, she is a goddess! |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Ha! |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Do reverence.<br>She is a goddess, [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman)<br>Of all [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]]<br>Methinks a [[motifs/rose\|rose]] is best. |
| WOMAN | Why, gentle madam? |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | It is the very emblem of a maid—<br>For when the west [[motifs/wind\|wind]] courts her gently,<br>How modestly she blows, and paints the sun<br>With her chaste blushes! When the north comes near<br>her,<br>Rude and impatient, then, like chastity,<br>She locks her beauties in her bud again,<br>And [[motifs/leaves\|leaves]] him to base briers. |
| WOMAN | Yet, good madam, Sometimes her modesty will blow so far<br>She falls for’t—a maid,<br>If she have any [[concepts/honour\|honour]], would be loath<br>To take example by her. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | Thou art wanton. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | (to [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]])<br>She is wondrous fair. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | She is all the [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] extant. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | (to her Woman)<br>The sun grows high—let’s walk in. Keep these [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]].<br>We’ll see how close art can come near their colours.<br>I am wondrous merry-hearted—I could laugh now. |
| WOMAN | I could lie down, I am sure. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | And take one with you? |
| WOMAN | That’s as we bargain, madam. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], agree then. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]] and her Woman |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | What think you of this [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]]? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | ’Tis a rare one. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Is’t but a rare one? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yes, a matchless [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]]. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Might not a man [[motifs/well\|well]] lose himself and [[motifs/love\|love]] her? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | I cannot tell what you have done; I have,<br>Beshrew mine eyes for’t. Now I feel my shackles. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | You [[motifs/love\|love]] her then? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Who would not? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | And [[motifs/desire\|desire]] her? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Before my liberty. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | I saw her first. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | That’s nothing. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | But it shall be. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | I saw her too. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes, but you must not [[motifs/love\|love]] her. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | I will not, as you do, to worship her<br>As she is heavenly and a blessèd goddess!<br>I [[motifs/love\|love]] her as a woman, to enjoy her—<br>So both may [[motifs/love\|love]]. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | You shall not [[motifs/love\|love]] at all. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Not [[motifs/love\|love]] at all—who shall deny me? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | I that first saw her, I that took possession<br>First with mine eye of all those beauties<br>In her revealed to mankind. If thou lov’st her,<br>Or entertain’st a [[concepts/hope\|hope]] to blast my wishes,<br>Thou art a traitor, [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]], and a fellow<br>False as thy title to her. [[concepts/friendship\|Friendship]], [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>And all the ties between us I disclaim,<br>If thou once think upon her. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yes, I [[motifs/love\|love]] her—And if the lives of all my name lay on it,<br>I must do so. I [[motifs/love\|love]] her with my [[motifs/soul\|soul]]—<br>If that will lose ye, farewell, [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]]!<br>I say again,<br>I [[motifs/love\|love]] her, and in loving her maintain<br>I am as worthy and as free a [[archetypes/lover\|lover]],<br>And have as just a title to her [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]],<br>As any [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]], or any living<br>That is a man’s son. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Have I called thee friend? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yes, and have found me so. Why are you moved<br>thus?<br>Let me deal coldly with you. Am not I<br>Part of your [[motifs/blood\|blood]], part of your [[motifs/soul\|soul]]? You have<br>told me<br>That I was [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] and you were [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Am not I liable to those affections,<br>Those joys, griefs, angers, fears, my friend shall<br>suffer? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Ye may be. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Why then would you deal so cunningly, So strangely, so unlike a noble kinsman,<br>To [[motifs/love\|love]] alone? Speak truly. Do you think me<br>Unworthy of her sight? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | No, but unjust If thou pursue that sight. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Because another First sees the enemy, shall I stand still,<br>And let mine [[concepts/honour\|honour]] down, and never charge? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Yes, if he be but one. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | But say that one<br>Had rather combat me? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Let that one say so, And use thy [[concepts/freedom\|freedom]]; else, if thou pursuest her,<br>Be as that cursed man that hates his country,<br>A branded [[archetypes/villain\|villain]]. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | You are mad. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | I must be. Till thou art worthy, [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]], it concerns me;<br>And in this [[concepts/madness\|madness]] if I hazard thee<br>And take thy life, I deal but truly. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Fie, sir. You play the [[archetypes/child\|child]] extremely. I will [[motifs/love\|love]] her,<br>I must, I ought to do so, and I dare—<br>And all this justly. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | O, that now, that now Thy false [[concepts/self\|self]] and thy friend had but this fortune—<br>To be one hour at liberty and grasp<br>Our good swords in our hands! I would quickly teach<br>thee<br>What ’twere to filch affection from another.<br>Thou art baser in it than a cutpurse.<br>Put but thy head out of this [[motifs/window\|window]] more<br>And, as I have a [[motifs/soul\|soul]], I’ll nail thy life to’t. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Thou dar’st not, [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]; thou canst not; thou art feeble.<br>Put my head out? I’ll throw my [[concepts/body\|body]] out<br>And leap the [[motifs/garden\|garden]] when I see her next, |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Jailer above |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | And pitch between her arms to anger thee. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | No more—the keeper’s coming. I shall live<br>To knock thy brains out with my shackles. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Do. |
| JAILER | By your leave, gentlemen. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Now, honest keeper? |
| JAILER | Lord [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]], you must presently to th’ Duke.<br>The cause I know not yet. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | I am ready, keeper. |
| JAILER | Prince [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]], I must a while bereave you<br>Of your fair cousin’s company. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] and the Jailer |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | And me, too, Even when you please, of life. Why is he sent for?<br>It may be he shall marry her—he’s goodly,<br>And like enough the Duke hath taken notice<br>Both of his [[motifs/blood\|blood]] and [[concepts/body\|body]]. But his falsehood!<br>Why should a friend be treacherous? If that<br>Get him a wife so noble and so fair,<br>Let honest men ne’er [[motifs/love\|love]] again. Once more<br>I would but see this fair one. Blessèd [[motifs/garden\|garden]],<br>And fruit and [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]] more blessed, that still blossom<br>As her bright eyes shine on ye! Would I were,<br>For all the fortune of my life hereafter,<br>Yon little tree, yon blooming apricot—<br>How I would spread and fling my wanton arms<br>In at her [[motifs/window\|window]]! I would bring her fruit<br>Fit for the gods to feed on; youth and pleasure<br>Still as she tasted should be doubled on her;<br>And if she be not heavenly, I would make her<br>So near the gods in [[concepts/nature\|nature]] they should [[concepts/fear\|fear]] her— |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Jailer bove |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | And then I am sure she would [[motifs/love\|love]] me. How now,<br>keeper,<br>Where’s [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]? |
| JAILER | Banished—Prince Pirithous Obtained his liberty; but never more,<br>Upon his oath and life, must he set foot<br>Upon this kingdom. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] aside |
| JAILER | He’s a blessed man. He shall see Thebes again, and call to arms<br>The bold young men that, when he bids ’em charge,<br>Fall on like [[motifs/fire\|fire]]. [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] shall have a fortune,<br>If he dare make himself a worthy [[archetypes/lover\|lover]],<br>Yet in the field to strike a battle for her;<br>And if he lose her then, he’s a cold coward.<br>How bravely may he bear himself to win her<br>If he be noble [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]; thousand ways!<br>Were I at liberty I would do things<br>Of such a virtuous [[concepts/greatness\|greatness]] that this lady,<br>This blushing virgin, should take manhood to her<br>And seek to ravish me. |
| JAILER | My lord, for you<br>I have this charge to— |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | To discharge my life. |
| JAILER | No, but from this place to remove your lordship—<br>The windows are too open. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Devils take ’em That are so envious to me—prithee kill me. |
| JAILER | And hang for’t afterward? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | By this good light, Had I as word I would kill thee. |
| JAILER | Why, my lord? |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Thou bring’st such pelting scurvy news continually,<br>Thou art not worthy life. I will not go. |
| JAILER | Indeed you must, my lord. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | May I see the [[motifs/garden\|garden]]? |
| JAILER | No. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Then I am resolved—I will not go. |
| JAILER | I must constrain you, then; and for you are dangerous,<br>I’ll clap more irons on you. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Do, good keeper. I’ll shake ’em so ye shall not [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]:<br>I’ll make ye a new morris. Must I go? |
| JAILER | There is no remedy. |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Farewell, kind [[motifs/window\|window]]. May rude [[motifs/wind\|wind]] never hurt thee. O, my lady,<br>If ever thou hast felt what sorrow was,<br>Dream how I suffer. Come, now bury me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] and the Jailer |

### Atto 2, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Banished the kingdom? ’Tis a benefit,<br>A [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]] I must thank ’em for; but banished<br>The free enjoying of that face I die for—<br>O, ‘twas a studied punishment, a [[motifs/death\|death]]<br>Beyond [[concepts/imagination\|imagination]]; such a vengeance<br>That, were I old and wicked, all my sins<br>Could never pluck upon me. [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]],<br>Thou hast the start now—thou shalt stay and see<br>Her bright eyes break each morning ’gainst thy<br>[[motifs/window\|window]],<br>And let in life into thee. Thou shalt feed<br>Upon the sweetness of a noble [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]]<br>That [[concepts/nature\|nature]] ne’er exceeded, nor ne’er shall.<br>Good gods! What happiness has [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]]!<br>Twenty to one he’ll come to speak to her,<br>And if she be as gentle as she’s fair,<br>I know she’s his—he has a tongue will tame<br>Tempests and make the wild rocks wanton.<br>Come what can come,<br>The worst is [[motifs/death\|death]]. I will not leave the kingdom.<br>I know mine own is but a heap of ruins,<br>And no redress there. If I go he has her.<br>I am resolved another shape shall make me,<br>Or end my fortunes. Either way I am happy—<br>I’ll see her and be near her, or no more. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter four Country People, one of whom carries a garland before them. [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] stands apart |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | My masters, I’ll be there—that’s certain. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | And I’ll be there. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | And I. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Why then, have with ye, boys! ’Tis but a chiding—<br>Let the plough play today, I’ll tickle’t out<br>Of the jades’ tails tomorrow. |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | I am sure<br>To have my wife as jealous as a turkey—<br>But that’s all one. I’ll go through, let her mumble. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | Clap her aboard tomorrow [[motifs/night\|night]] and stow her,<br>And all’s made up again. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | Ay, do but put A fescue in her fist and you shall see her<br>Take a new lesson out and be a good wench.<br>Do we all hold against the maying? |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Hold? What should ail us? |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | Areas will be there. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | And Sennois, and Rycas, and three better lads ne’er danced under green tree; and ye know what wenches, ha? But will the dainty dominie, the schoolmaster, keep touch, do you think? For he does all, ye know. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | He’ll eat a hornbook ere he fail. Go to, the matter’s too far driven between him and the tanner’s daughter to let slip now, and she must see the Duke, and she must [[motifs/dance\|dance]] too. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Shall we be lusty? |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | All the boys in Athens blow [[motifs/wind\|wind]] i’th’ breech on’s! And here I’ll be and there I’ll be, for our town, and here again and there again—ha, boys, hey for the weavers! |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | This must be done i’th’ woods. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | O, pardon me. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | By any means, our thing of learning said so; where he himself will edify the Duke most parlously in our behalfs—he’s excellent i’th’ woods, bring him to th’ plains, his learning makes no cry. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | We’ll see the sports, then every man to’s tackle—and, sweet companions, let’s rehearse, by any means, before the ladies see us, and do sweetly, and God knows what may come on’t. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Content—the sports once ended, we’ll perform. Away boys, and hold. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | (coming forward) By your [[motifs/leaves\|leaves]], honest friends, pray you whither go you? 6 |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | Whither? Why, what a question’s that? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Yet ’tis a question<br>To me that know not. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | To the games, my friend. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | Where were you bred, you know it not? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Not far, sir<br>Are there such games today? |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | Yes, marry, are there, And such as you never saw. The Duke himself<br>Will be in person there. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | What pastimes are they? |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | Wrestling and running. (To the others) ’Tis a pretty fellow. |
| THIRD COUNTRYMAN | (to [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]])<br>Thou wilt not go along? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Not yet, sir. |
| FOURTH COUNTRYMAN | [[motifs/well\|Well]], sir, Take your own [[motifs/time\|time]]. (To the others) Come, boys. |
| FIRST COUNTRYMAN | My mind misgives me—This fellow has a vengeance trick o’th’ hip:<br>Mark how his [[concepts/body\|body]]’s made for’t. |
| SECOND COUNTRYMAN | I’ll be hanged though If he dare venture; hang him, plum porridge!<br>He wrestle? He roast eggs! Come, let’s be gone, lads. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the four Countrymen |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | This is an offered opportunity<br>I durst not wish for. [[motifs/well\|Well]] I could have wrestled—<br>The best men called it excellent—and run<br>Swifter than [[motifs/wind\|wind]] upon a field of corn,<br>Curling the wealthy ears, never flew. I’ll venture,<br>And in some poor disguise be there. Who knows<br>Whether my brows may not be girt with garlands,<br>And happiness prefer me to a place<br>Where I may ever dwell in sight of her? Exit |

### Atto 2, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Jailer’s Daughter |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | Why should I [[motifs/love\|love]] this gentleman? ’Tis odds<br>He never will affect me. I am base,<br>My father the mean keeper of his [[motifs/prison\|prison]],<br>And he a prince. To marry him is hopeless,<br>To be his whore is witless. Out upon’t,<br>What pushes are we wenches driven to<br>When fifteen once has found us? First, I saw him;<br>I, seeing, thought he was a goodly man;<br>He has as much to please a woman in him—<br>If he please to bestow it so—as ever<br>These eyes yet looked on. Next, I pitied him,<br>And so would any young wench, o‘my [[concepts/conscience\|conscience]],<br>That ever dreamed or vowed her maidenhead<br>To a young handsome man. Then, I loved him,<br>Extremely loved him, infinitely loved him—<br>And yet he had a cousin fair as he, too.<br>But in my heart was [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]], and there,<br>Lord, what a coil he keeps! To hear him<br>Sing in an evening, what a heaven it is!<br>And yet his songs are sad ones. Fairer spoken<br>Was never gentleman. When I come in<br>To bring him [[motifs/water\|water]] in a morning, first<br>He bows his noble [[concepts/body\|body]], then salutes me, thus:<br>‘Fair, gentle maid, good morrow. May thy goodness<br>Get thee a happy husband.’ Once he kissed me—<br>I loved my lips the better ten days after.<br>Would he would do so every day! He grieves much,<br>And me as much to see his misery.<br>What should I do to make him know I [[motifs/love\|love]] him?<br>For I would fain enjoy him. Say I ventured<br>To set him free? What says the law then? Thus much<br>For law or kindred! I will do it,<br>And this [[motifs/night\|night]]; ere tomorrow he shall [[motifs/love\|love]] me. Exit |

### Atto 2, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Short flourish of cornetts and shouts within. Enter [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]], [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]], Pirithous, [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]], [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] disguised, with a garland, and attendants |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | You have done worthily. I have not seen<br>Since Hercules a man of tougher sinews.<br>Whate’er you are, you run the best and wrestle<br>That these times can allow. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | I am proud to please you. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | What country bred you? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | This—but far off, prince. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Are you a gentleman? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | My father said so, And to those gentle uses gave me life. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Are you his heir? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | His youngest, sir. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Your father Sure is a happy sire, then. What proves you? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | A little of all noble qualities.<br>I could have kept a hawk and [[motifs/well\|well]] have hollered<br>To a deep cry of dogs; I dare not praise<br>My feat in horsemanship, yet they that knew me<br>Would say it was my best piece; last and greatest,<br>I would be thought a soldier. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | You are perfect. |
| PIRITHOIIS | Upon my [[motifs/soul\|soul]], a proper man. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | He is so. |
| PIRITHOUS | (to [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]])<br>How do you like him, lady? |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | I admire him. I have not seen so young a man so noble—<br>If he say true—of his sort. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | Believe His mother was a wondrous handsome woman—<br>His face methinks goes that way. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | But his [[concepts/body\|body]] And fiery mind illustrate a brave father. |
| PIRITHOUS | Mark how his virtue, like a hidden sun,<br>Breaks through his baser garments. |
| [[characters/hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | He’s [[motifs/well\|well]] got, sure. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | (to [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]])<br>What made you seek this place, sir? |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Noble [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]], To purchase name and do my ablest service<br>To such a [[motifs/well\|well]]-found [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]] as thy worth,<br>For only in thy [[settings/court\|court]] of all the world<br>Dwells fair-eyed [[concepts/honour\|honour]]. |
| PIRITHOUS | All his words are worthy. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | (to [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]])<br>Sir, we are much indebted to your travel,<br>Nor shall you lose your wish.—Pirithous,<br>Dispose of this fair gentleman. |
| PIRITHOUS | Thanks, [[characters/theseus\|Theseus]]. (To [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]) Whate’er you are, you’re mine, and I shall<br>give you<br>To a most noble service, to this lady,<br>This bright young virgin; pray observe her goodness.<br>You have honoured her fair birthday with your<br>virtues,<br>And as your due you’re hers. Kiss her fair hand, sir. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Sir, you’re a noble giver. (To [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]]) Dearest [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]],<br>Thus let me seal my vowed [[concepts/faith\|faith]].<br>He kisses her hand<br>When your servant,<br>Your most unworthy creature, but offends you,<br>Command him die, he shall. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | That were too cruel. If you deserve [[motifs/well\|well]], sir, I shall soon see’t.<br>You’re mine, and somewhat better than your rank I’ll<br>use you. |
| PIRITHOUS | (to [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]])<br>I’ll see you furnished, and, because you say<br>You are a horseman, I must needs entreat you<br>This afternoon to ride—but ’tis a rough one. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | I like him better, prince—I shall not then<br>Freeze in my saddle. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | (to [[characters/hippolyta\|Hippolyta]]) |
| *(didascalia)* | Sweet, you must be ready—And you, [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]], to Pirithous] and you, friend—and |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | all,<br>Tomorrow by the sun, to do observance<br>To flow’ry May in Dian’s wood. (To [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]) Wait [[motifs/well\|well]],<br>sir,<br>Upon your mistress.—Emity, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]]<br>He shall not go afoot. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | That were a shame, sir, While I have horses. (To [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]]) Take your choice, and<br>what<br>You want, at any [[motifs/time\|time]], let me but know it.<br>If you serve faithfully, I dare assure you,<br>You’ll find a loving mistress. |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | If I do not, Let me find that my father ever hated—<br>Disgrace and blows. |
| [[characters/theseus\|THESEUS]] | Go, lead the way—you have won it. It shall be so: you shall receive all dues<br>Fit for the [[concepts/honour\|honour]] you have won. ’Twere wrong else.<br>(To [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]]) Sister, beshrew my heart, you have a<br>servant<br>That, if I were a woman, would be master.<br>But you are wise. |
| [[characters/emilia\|EMILIA]] | I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] too wise for that, sir. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the jailer’s Daughter |
| JAILER’S DAUGHTER | Let all the dukes and all the devils roar—<br>He is at liberty! I have ventured for him,<br>And out I have brought him. To a little wood<br>A mile hence I have sent him, where a cedar<br>Higher than all the rest spreads like a plane,<br>Fast by a brook—and there he shall keep close<br>Till I provide him files and food, for yet<br>His iron bracelets are not off. O [[motifs/love\|Love]],<br>What a stout-hearted [[archetypes/child\|child]] thou art! My father<br>Durst better have endured cold iron than done it.<br>I [[motifs/love\|love]] him beyond [[motifs/love\|love]] and beyond reason<br>Or wit or safety. I have made him know it—<br>I care not, I am desperate. If the law<br>Find me and then condemn me for‘t, some wenches,<br>Some honest-hearted maids, will sing my [[forms/dirge\|dirge]]<br>And tell to [[motifs/memory\|memory]] my [[motifs/death\|death]] was noble,<br>Dying almost a martyr. That way he takes,<br>I purpose, is my way too. Sure, he cannot<br>Be so unmanly as to leave me here.<br>If he do, maids will not so easily<br>Trust men again. And yet, he has not thanked me<br>For what I have done—no, not so much as kissed me—<br>And that, methinks, is not so [[motifs/well\|well]]. Nor scarcely<br>Could I persuade him to become a free man,<br>He made such scruples of the wrong he did<br>To me and to my father. Yet, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]]<br>When he considers more, this [[motifs/love\|love]] of mine<br>Will take more root within him. Let him do<br>What he will with me—so he use me kindly.<br>For use me, so he shall, or I’ll proclaim him,<br>And to his face, no man. I’ll presently<br>Provide him necessaries and pack my clothes up,<br>And where there is a patch of ground I’ll venture,<br>So he be with me. By him, like a [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]],<br>I’ll ever dwell. Within this hour the hubbub<br>Will be all o’er the [[motifs/prison\|prison]]—I am then<br>Kissing the man they look for. Farewell, father:<br>Get many more such prisoners and such daughters,<br>And shortly you may keep yourself. Now to him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | A bush in place. Cornetts in sundry places. Noise and hollering as of people a-Maying. Enter [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | The Duke has lost Hippo)yta—each took<br>A several laund. This is a solemn rite<br>They owe bloomed May, and the Athenians pay it<br>To th’ heart of ceremony. O, [[characters/queen\|Queen]] [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]],<br>Fresher than May, sweeter<br>Than her gold buttons on the boughs, or all<br>Th’enamelled knacks o’th’ mead or [[motifs/garden\|garden]]—yea,<br>We challenge too the bank of any nymph<br>That makes the stream seem [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]]; thou, O [[motifs/jewel\|jewel]]<br>O’th’ wood, o’th’ world, hast likewise blessed a pace |
| *(didascalia)* | With thy sole presence in thy |
| *(didascalia)* |  rumination |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | That I, poor man, might eftsoons come between<br>And chop on some cold thought. Thrice blessèd<br>chance<br>To drop on such a mistress, expectation<br>Most guiltless on‘t! Tell me, O Lady Fortune,<br>Next after Emily my sovereign, how far<br>I may be proud. She takes strong note of me,<br>Hath made me near her, and this beauteous morn,<br>The prim’st of all the year, presents me with<br>A brace of horses—two such steeds might [[motifs/well\|well]]<br>Be by a pair of kings backed, in a field<br>That their crowns’ titles tried. Alas, alas,<br>Poor cousin [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]], poor prisoner—thou<br>So little dream’st upon my fortune that<br>Thou think’st thyself the happier thing to be<br>So near [[characters/emilia\|Emilia]]. Me thou deem’st at Thebes,<br>And therein wretched, although free. But if<br>Thou knew’st my mistress breathed on me, and that<br>I eared her language, lived in her eye-O, coz,<br>What passion would enclose thee! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]] as out of a bush with his shackles. He bends his fist at [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]] |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Traitor kinsman, Thou shouldst perceive my passion if these signs<br>Of prisonment were off me, and this hand<br>But owner of a sword. By all oaths in one,<br>I and the justice of my [[motifs/love\|love]] would make thee<br>A confessed traitor. O thou most perfidious<br>That ever gently looked, the void’st of [[concepts/honour\|honour]]<br>That e’er bore gentle token, falsest cousin<br>That ever [[motifs/blood\|blood]] made kin—call’st thou her thine?<br>I’ll prove it in my shackles, with these hands,<br>Void of appointment, that thou liest and art<br>A very thief in [[motifs/love\|love]], a chaffy lord<br>Not worth the name of [[archetypes/villain\|villain]]. Had I as word<br>And these house-clogs away— |
| [[characters/arcite\|ARCITE]] | Dear cousin [[characters/palamon\|Palamon]]— |
| [[characters/palamon\|PALAMON]] | Cozener [[characters/arcite\|Arcite]], give me language such<br>As thou hast showed me feat. |
